Yeah… I mean, not like I have some plan to do anything to myself. It’s not like that, exactly.

the senior guys, like I was about to change how every building in Miami handled water if people would just listen.\n- key: S037\n  name: Pressured_Speech\n  description: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.\n  manifestation: In class and at the shop I would start explaining an idea and just keep going, talking over people and barely taking a breath even when I could see they I’m not sure… two weeks feels like a really specific amount of time. I’ve had better days, definitely, like where I get through work and I’m not overthinking every little thing as much.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They kind of come and go, but it feels like they’re around a lot. Like, what made me finally come in was I kept feeling like I’d ruined things for everybody — like I’m this fake in the apprenticeship and sooner or later everyone’s going to figure it out.

And then I start thinking I let my family down, or the younger apprentices too, which sounds dramatic because it’ll be over stuff other people would probably just fix and move on from. Like if something goes wrong on a job, or I don’t catch something fast enough, I’ll replay it way too much. So it’s not every second, but it keeps coming back.

It turns into this whole thing where I’m like, I ruined things for everybody.\n\nLike at the apprenticeship, I keep feeling like I’m some kind of fake and everyone’s going to realize I shouldn’t be there. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more… intermittent, I guess, but when it shows up it can take over a big chunk of the day. Like I’ll mess up something small at the apprenticeship, or even just think I did, and then I’m stuck feeling like I ruined things for everybody.

And then my brain goes off like, “You’re a fake, you don’t belong here,” and I start thinking I let my family down, or even the younger apprentices, which is stupid because they’re not even— I mean, they probably don’t think about me that much. But it feels really big in the moment.

So not literally every minute all day, but it keeps coming back and it’s hard to shake once it starts.

It only got harder to pretend once school and work started slipping, because then people could actually see I wasn’t keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness or guilt been constant over the past nine months, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They kind of come and go, but it feels like they’re around a lot. Like, what made me finally come in was I kept feeling like I’d ruined things for everybody — like I’m this fake in the apprenticeship and sooner or later everyone’s going to figure it out.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I let my family down, or the younger apprentices too, which sounds dramatic because it’ll be over stuff other people would probably just fix and move on from. Like if something goes wrong on a job, or I don’t catch something fast enough, I’ll replay it way too much. So it’s not every second, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of worthlessness or guilt something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s more… intermittent, I guess, but when it shows up it can take over a big chunk of the day. Like I’ll mess up something small at the apprenticeship, or even just think I did, and then I’m stuck feeling like I ruined things for everybody.\n\nAnd then my brain goes off like, “You’re a fake, you don’t belong here,” and I start thinking I let my family down, or even the younger apprentices, which is stupid because they’re not even— I mean, they probably don’t think about me that much. But it feels really big in the moment.\n\nSo not literally every minute all day, but it keeps coming back and it’s hard to shake once it starts.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you ever have a period where you felt significantly better than you do now, even if it didn't last?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 20 / plumber_pipefitter_or_steamfitter / associates\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\nmanifestation: \"There were weeks where I felt almost too good, like Miami was mine and every little comment from a coworker who slowed me down made me snap way harder than I normally would.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… there were some weeks where I felt way better, almost too good, honestly. Like, I’d be walking around feeling like Miami was mine, like everything was just clicking.

But it wasn’t just “happy,” I guess. If a coworker made some little comment or slowed me down on a job, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. And then later I’d be like, why did I react like that? But in the moment it felt completely justified.

Yeah, definitely more energy. It was like this charge in my body that wouldn’t shut off. I’d do a full day on pipes, be tired like I should be tired, but then I’d still go to evening class and come home and reorganize all my tools.

But I keep thinking I let my family down, and even the younger apprentices, which sounds weird because I’m not, like, in charge of them or anything. It just sits on me like I ruined things.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling like you've messed everything up for everyone?\n\nPatient: Probably… around nine months? I mean, it kind of crept in, so I didn’t really mark the date or anything. At first I thought I was just stressed, like everyone in my program is stressed, and there’s always some guy saying he only slept three hours and still showed up, which makes you feel like you’re being dramatic.\n\nBut yeah, this stretch has been there for about nine months. It got harder to pretend I was fine once school and work started slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, I guess. Like, I still have the stuff there — my sketchbook is literally on my desk, and I’ll walk past it and think, “I should do something,” but then I just don’t feel anything about it.\n\nSame with volleyball. I used to actually look forward to going, even if I was tired from work, because it felt like… mine, you know? And there’s this volunteer group chat I’m in, and I used to answer right away, but lately I just let the texts sit there. It’s weird because those were the things that made me feel like myself, and now I kind of just ignore them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t really weigh myself much. My work pants still fit about the same, I think, though sometimes they feel different depending on the day, but that might just be laundry or, like, the waistband on those things is awful.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve noticed a big weight drop, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or guilty about things beyond just making mistakes at your apprenticeship?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the main thing, I think. It’s not just like, “oh, I messed up that fitting” or whatever. It turns into this whole thing where I’m like, I ruined things for everybody.\n\nLike at the apprenticeship, I keep feeling like I’m some kind of fake and everyone’s going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And then I think about my family, and how they were proud I got into it, and I feel like I’m letting them down too. Even with the younger apprentices, which is stupid because I’m only 20, but I’ll think I’m setting a bad example or making things harder for them.\n\nAnd I know, logically, some of the stuff is stuff other people would probably just fix and move on from. But I don’t. I just carry it around and it feels like it proves something bad about me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about death or dying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I have some plan to do anything to myself. It’s not like that, exactly.\n\nBut sometimes, like when I’m driving home after class — and the road is just the same road every night, and I’m thinking about all the stuff I didn’t get done, or how I probably seemed weird that day — my brain kind of goes, it would be easier if I just… didn’t wake up tomorrow.\n\nOr when I’m lying awake, I’ll think that too. Not that I’m about to do something, just that being here feels really heavy sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like hearing someone talking when nobody’s there, or seeing a person in the room or anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I hear my name, but then it’s like the pipes in the building or someone down the hall — the shop is loud, you know, with all the metal and banging and people yelling over tools. So I don’t really know if that counts, but I don’t think I’ve had… like, voices or visions, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt better and your symptoms were not as severe?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… two weeks feels like a really specific amount of time. I haven’t noticed it that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were back to your normal self for a little while during these nine months?\n\nPatient: Not really “normal,” no. I mean, there were little pockets where I could kind of act normal, like get through the day and make jokes or whatever, but it was still sort of there underneath.\n\nIt’s been going on for… I guess around nine months? Which sounds weird when I say it out loud, because I kept thinking it was just a rough week, then another rough week. It only got harder to pretend once school and work started slipping, because then people could actually see I wasn’t keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness or guilt been constant over the past nine months, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They kind of come and go, but it feels like they’re around a lot. Like, what made me finally come in was I kept feeling like I’d ruined things for everybody — like I’m this fake in the apprenticeship and sooner or later everyone’s going to figure it out.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I let my family down, or the younger apprentices too, which sounds dramatic because it’ll be over stuff other people would probably just fix and move on from. Not that I’m about to do something, just that being here feels really heavy sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like hearing someone talking when nobody’s there, or seeing a person in the room or anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I hear my name, but then it’s like the pipes in the building or someone down the hall — the shop is loud, you know, with all the metal and banging and people yelling over tools. So I don’t really know if that counts, but I don’t think I’ve had… like, voices or visions, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt better and your symptoms were not as severe?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… two weeks feels like a really specific amount of time. I’ve had better days, definitely, like where I get through work and I’m not overthinking every little thing as much.\n\nBut then I’ll have a bad night or something and it kind of ruins my sense of how long it was going okay. I’m bad at tracking time too — I always mean to use one of those calendar apps, but then I forget, and my phone is full of reminders I just swipe away.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t really say, like, yes, there was a solid two-week stretch where everything was clearly better. I haven’t noticed it that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were back to your normal self for a little while during these nine months?\n\nPatient: Not really “normal,” no. I mean, there were little pockets where I could kind of act normal, like get through the day and make jokes or whatever, but it was still sort of there underneath.\n\nIt’s been going on for… I guess around nine months? Which sounds weird when I say it out loud, because I kept thinking it was just a rough week, then another rough week. It only got harder to pretend once school and work started slipping, because then people could actually see I wasn’t keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness or guilt been constant over the past nine months, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They kind of come and go, but it feels like they’re around a lot. Like, what made me finally come in was I kept feeling like I’d ruined things for everybody — like I’m this fake in the apprenticeship and sooner or later everyone’s going to figure it out.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I let my family down, or the younger apprentices too, which sounds dramatic because it’ll be over stuff other people would probably just fix and move on from. Like if something goes wrong on a job, or I don’t catch something fast enough, I’ll replay it way too much. So it’s not every second, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of worthlessness or guilt something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s more… intermittent, I guess, but when it shows up it can take over a big chunk of the day. Like I’ll mess up something small at the apprenticeship, or even just think I did, and then I’m stuck feeling like I ruined things for everybody.\n\nAnd then my brain goes off like, “You’re a fake, you don’t belong here,” and I start thinking I let my family down, or even the younger apprentices, which is stupid because they’re not even— I mean, they probably don’t think about me that much. But it feels really big in the moment.\n\nSo not literally every minute all day, but it keeps coming back and it’s hard to shake once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have a period where you felt significantly better than you do now, even if it didn't last?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were some weeks where I felt way better, almost too good, honestly. Like, I’d be walking around feeling like Miami was mine, like everything was just clicking.\n\nBut it wasn’t just “happy,” I guess. If a coworker made some little comment or slowed me down on a job, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. And then later I’d be like, why did I react like that? But in the moment it felt completely justified.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you have a lot more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely more energy. It was like this charge in my body that wouldn’t shut off. I’d do a full day on pipes, be tired like I should be tired, but then I’d still go to evening class and come home and reorganize all my tools.\n\nAnd then I’d be cleaning my apartment at like— not even because it was that messy, just because I had to be doing something. Or I’d start drawing out systems and plans instead of sitting down. My roommate used to joke about how I can’t even watch one episode of something without getting up, but during those weeks it was way more than that.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt way better, did you find yourself getting irritable or angry more easily than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. More than usual. It’s like when I was in those weeks where I felt almost too good—like everything was going my way, like the whole city was mine or something—then little things would get under my skin really fast.\n\nLike if a coworker made a comment or slowed me down on a job, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. And then I’d be thinking about it after, like, why did I make that such a big deal? But at the time it felt like they were in my way, you know? Like I couldn’t just let it go.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… yes. At first I thought I was just stressed, like everyone in my program is stressed, and there’s always some guy saying he only slept three hours and still showed up, which makes you feel like you’re being dramatic.\n\nBut yeah, this stretch has been there for about nine months. It got harder to pretend I was fine once school and work started slipping.\n\nDoctor: Do you still find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, I guess. Like, I still have the stuff there — my sketchbook is literally on my desk, and I’ll walk past it and think, “I should do something,” but then I just don’t feel anything about it.\n\nSame with volleyball. I used to actually look forward to going, even if I was tired from work, because it felt like… mine, you know? And there’s this volunteer group chat I’m in, and I used to answer right away, but lately I just let the texts sit there. It’s weird because those were the things that made me feel like myself, and now I kind of just ignore them.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past nine months?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t really weigh myself much. My work pants still fit about the same, I think, though sometimes they feel different depending on the day, but that might just be laundry or, like, the waistband on those things is awful.\n\nSo… I don’t think I’ve noticed a big weight drop, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless or guilty about things beyond just making mistakes at your apprenticeship?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, that’s kind of the main thing, I think. It’s not just like, “oh, I messed up that fitting” or whatever. It turns into this whole thing where I’m like, I ruined things for everybody.\n\nLike at the apprenticeship, I keep feeling like I’m some kind of fake and everyone’s going to realize I shouldn’t be there. And then I think about my family, and how they were proud I got into it, and I feel like I’m letting them down too. Even with the younger apprentices, which is stupid because I’m only 20, but I’ll think I’m setting a bad example or making things harder for them.\n\nAnd I know, logically, some of the stuff is stuff other people would probably just fix and move on from. But I don’t. I just carry it around and it feels like it proves something bad about me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about death or dying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I have some plan to do anything to myself. It’s not like that, exactly.\n\nBut sometimes, like when I’m driving home after class — and the road is just the same road every night, and I’m thinking about all the stuff I didn’t get done, or how I probably seemed weird that day — my brain kind of goes, it would be easier if I just… didn’t wake up tomorrow.\n\nOr when I’m lying awake, I’ll think that too. Not that I’m about to do something, just that being here feels really heavy sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like hearing someone talking when nobody’s there, or seeing a person in the room or anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I hear my name, but then it’s like the pipes in the building or someone down the hall — the shop is loud, you know, with all the metal and banging and people yelling over tools. So I don’t really know if that counts, but I don’t think I’ve had… like, voices or visions, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt better and your symptoms were not as severe?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… two weeks feels like a really specific amount of time. I’ve had better days, definitely, like where I get through work and I’m not overthinking every little thing as much.\n\nBut then I’ll have a bad night or something and it kind of ruins my sense of how long it was going okay. I’m bad at tracking time too — I always mean to use one of those calendar apps, but then I forget, and my phone is full of reminders I just swipe away.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t really say, like, yes, there was a solid two-week stretch where everything was clearly better. I haven’t noticed it that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were back to your normal self for a little while during these nine months?\n\nPatient: Not really “normal,” no. I mean, there were little pockets where I could kind of act normal, like get through the day and make jokes or whatever, but it was still sort of there underneath.\n\nIt’s been going on for… I guess around nine months? Which sounds weird when I say it out loud, because I kept thinking it was just a rough week, then another rough week. It only got harder to pretend once school and work started slipping, because then people could actually see I wasn’t keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness or guilt been constant over the past nine months, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They kind of come and go, but it feels like they’re around a lot. Like, what made me finally come in was I kept feeling like I’d ruined things for everybody — like I’m this fake in the apprenticeship and sooner or later everyone’s going to figure it out.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I let my family down, or the younger apprentices too, which sounds dramatic because it’ll be over stuff other people would probably just fix and move on from. Like if something goes wrong on a job, or I don’t catch something fast enough, I’ll replay it way too much. So it’s not every second, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of worthlessness or guilt something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s more… intermittent, I guess, but when it shows up it can take over a big chunk of the day. Like I’ll mess up something small at the apprenticeship, or even just think I did, and then I’m stuck feeling like I ruined things for everybody.\n\nAnd then my brain goes off like, “You’re a fake, you don’t belong here,” and I start thinking I let my family down, or even the younger apprentices, which is stupid because they’re not even— I mean, they probably don’t think about me that much. But it feels really big in the moment.\n\nSo not literally every minute all day, but it keeps coming back and it’s hard to shake once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have a period where you felt significantly better than you do now, even if it didn't last?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were some weeks where I felt way better, almost too good, honestly. Like, I’d be walking around feeling like Miami was mine, like everything was just clicking.\n\nBut it wasn’t just “happy,” I guess. If a coworker made some little comment or slowed me down on a job, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. And then later I’d be like, why did I react like that? But in the moment it felt completely justified.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you have a lot more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely more energy. It was like this charge in my body that wouldn’t shut off. I’d do a full day on pipes, be tired like I should be tired, but then I’d still go to evening class and come home and reorganize all my tools.\n\nAnd then I’d be cleaning my apartment at like— not even because it was that messy, just because I had to be doing something. Or I’d start drawing out systems and plans instead of sitting down. My roommate used to joke about how I can’t even watch one episode of something without getting up, but during those weeks it was way more than that.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt way better, did you find yourself getting irritable or angry more easily than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. More than usual. It’s like when I was in those weeks where I felt almost too good—like everything was going my way, like the whole city was mine or something—then little things would get under my skin really fast.\n\nLike if a coworker made a comment or slowed me down on a job, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. And then I’d be thinking about it after, like, why did I make that such a big deal? But at the time it felt like they were in my way, you know? Like I couldn’t just let it go.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… yes. For a while I was only sleeping like three or four hours, and somehow I’d still show up to the job all wired, like ready to go, and then still make it to class after.\n\nIt was weird because normally if I sleep like that, I’m useless. But then I was just… up. And now it’s different, like I either can’t fall asleep or I wake up before my alarm and feel like I didn’t rest at all. My alarm is one of those annoying ones too, the kind that makes you want to throw your phone, but lately I’m already awake before it even starts.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you ever feel like you were engaging in risky behaviors or activities that you might not normally do?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say “risky,” exactly, but I guess I’m not totally sure what counts.\n\nLike, I wasn’t out doing anything wild on purpose or anything. Mostly I was just busy and feeling like I could handle everything. Sometimes people act like just being out late is risky, but with work and school, late is kind of normal for me. My uncle used to say tradespeople keep vampire hours sometimes, which isn’t really true, but—sorry.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear example of something I did that I’d call risky.\n\nDoctor: When you felt way better during those weeks, was it like a switch had been flipped or did it come on more gradually?\n\nPatient: It felt kind of like a switch, yeah. Not like one exact second, maybe, but I’d realize I was in it and suddenly everything felt bigger and better, like Miami was mine or something.\n\nAnd then at work, if somebody slowed me down or made some little comment, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. Which is funny because some of those guys talk all day anyway—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yeah, it didn’t feel like my normal mood gradually improving. It felt more like I’d flipped into this other mode for a few weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. So I don’t really know if that counts, but I don’t think I’ve had… like, voices or visions, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt better and your symptoms were not as severe?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… two weeks feels like a really specific amount of time. I’ve had better days, definitely, like where I get through work and I’m not overthinking every little thing as much.\n\nBut then I’ll have a bad night or something and it kind of ruins my sense of how long it was going okay. Which sounds weird when I say it out loud, because I kept thinking it was just a rough week, then another rough week. It only got harder to pretend once school and work started slipping, because then people could actually see I wasn’t keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness or guilt been constant over the past nine months, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They kind of come and go, but it feels like they’re around a lot. Like, what made me finally come in was I kept feeling like I’d ruined things for everybody — like I’m this fake in the apprenticeship and sooner or later everyone’s going to figure it out.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I let my family down, or the younger apprentices too, which sounds dramatic because it’ll be over stuff other people would probably just fix and move on from. Like if something goes wrong on a job, or I don’t catch something fast enough, I’ll replay it way too much. So it’s not every second, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of worthlessness or guilt something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s more… intermittent, I guess, but when it shows up it can take over a big chunk of the day. Like I’ll mess up something small at the apprenticeship, or even just think I did, and then I’m stuck feeling like I ruined things for everybody.\n\nAnd then my brain goes off like, “You’re a fake, you don’t belong here,” and I start thinking I let my family down, or even the younger apprentices, which is stupid because they’re not even— I mean, they probably don’t think about me that much. But it feels really big in the moment.\n\nSo not literally every minute all day, but it keeps coming back and it’s hard to shake once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have a period where you felt significantly better than you do now, even if it didn't last?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were some weeks where I felt way better, almost too good, honestly. So I don’t really know if that counts, but I don’t think I’ve had… like, voices or visions, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt better and your symptoms were not as severe?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… two weeks feels like a really specific amount of time. Like, what made me finally come in was I kept feeling like I’d ruined things for everybody — like I’m this fake in the apprenticeship and sooner or later everyone’s going to figure it out.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I let my family down, or the younger apprentices too, which sounds dramatic because it’ll be over stuff other people would probably just fix and move on from. More than usual. Even with the younger apprentices, which is stupid because I’m only 20, but I’ll think I’m setting a bad example or making things harder for them.\n\nAnd I know, logically, some of the stuff is stuff other people would probably just fix and move on from. But I don’t. I just carry it around and it feels like it proves something bad about me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever thought about death or dying?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not like I have some plan to do anything to myself. It’s not like that, exactly.\n\nBut sometimes, like when I’m driving home after class — and the road is just the same road every night, and I’m thinking about all the stuff I didn’t get done, or how I probably seemed weird that day — my brain kind of goes, it would be easier if I just… didn’t wake up tomorrow.\n\nOr when I’m lying awake, I’ll think that too. Not that I’m about to do something, just that being here feels really heavy sometimes.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that other people couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like hearing someone talking when nobody’s there, or seeing a person in the room or anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll think I hear my name, but then it’s like the pipes in the building or someone down the hall — the shop is loud, you know, with all the metal and banging and people yelling over tools. So I don’t really know if that counts, but I don’t think I’ve had… like, voices or visions, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a period of at least two weeks where you felt better and your symptoms were not as severe?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… two weeks feels like a really specific amount of time. I’ve had better days, definitely, like where I get through work and I’m not overthinking every little thing as much.\n\nBut then I’ll have a bad night or something and it kind of ruins my sense of how long it was going okay. I’m bad at tracking time too — I always mean to use one of those calendar apps, but then I forget, and my phone is full of reminders I just swipe away.\n\nSo… maybe? But I can’t really say, like, yes, there was a solid two-week stretch where everything was clearly better. I haven’t noticed it that cleanly.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever feel like you were back to your normal self for a little while during these nine months?\n\nPatient: Not really “normal,” no. I mean, there were little pockets where I could kind of act normal, like get through the day and make jokes or whatever, but it was still sort of there underneath.\n\nIt’s been going on for… I guess around nine months? Which sounds weird when I say it out loud, because I kept thinking it was just a rough week, then another rough week. It only got harder to pretend once school and work started slipping, because then people could actually see I wasn’t keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of worthlessness or guilt been constant over the past nine months, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: They kind of come and go, but it feels like they’re around a lot. Like, what made me finally come in was I kept feeling like I’d ruined things for everybody — like I’m this fake in the apprenticeship and sooner or later everyone’s going to figure it out.\n\nAnd then I start thinking I let my family down, or the younger apprentices too, which sounds dramatic because it’ll be over stuff other people would probably just fix and move on from. Like if something goes wrong on a job, or I don’t catch something fast enough, I’ll replay it way too much. So it’s not every second, but it keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Are these feelings of worthlessness or guilt something you experience for most of the day, or are they more intermittent?\n\nPatient: It’s more… intermittent, I guess, but when it shows up it can take over a big chunk of the day. Like I’ll mess up something small at the apprenticeship, or even just think I did, and then I’m stuck feeling like I ruined things for everybody.\n\nAnd then my brain goes off like, “You’re a fake, you don’t belong here,” and I start thinking I let my family down, or even the younger apprentices, which is stupid because they’re not even— I mean, they probably don’t think about me that much. But it feels really big in the moment.\n\nSo not literally every minute all day, but it keeps coming back and it’s hard to shake once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have a period where you felt significantly better than you do now, even if it didn't last?\n\nPatient: Yeah… there were some weeks where I felt way better, almost too good, honestly. Like, I’d be walking around feeling like Miami was mine, like everything was just clicking.\n\nBut it wasn’t just “happy,” I guess. If a coworker made some little comment or slowed me down on a job, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. And then later I’d be like, why did I react like that? But in the moment it felt completely justified.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you have a lot more energy than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely more energy. It was like this charge in my body that wouldn’t shut off. I’d do a full day on pipes, be tired like I should be tired, but then I’d still go to evening class and come home and reorganize all my tools.\n\nAnd then I’d be cleaning my apartment at like— not even because it was that messy, just because I had to be doing something. Or I’d start drawing out systems and plans instead of sitting down. My roommate used to joke about how I can’t even watch one episode of something without getting up, but during those weeks it was way more than that.\n\nDoctor: During those times when you felt way better, did you find yourself getting irritable or angry more easily than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. More than usual. It’s like when I was in those weeks where I felt almost too good—like everything was going my way, like the whole city was mine or something—then little things would get under my skin really fast.\n\nLike if a coworker made a comment or slowed me down on a job, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. And then I’d be thinking about it after, like, why did I make that such a big deal? But at the time it felt like they were in my way, you know? Like I couldn’t just let it go.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you find yourself needing less sleep than usual?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… yes. For a while I was only sleeping like three or four hours, and somehow I’d still show up to the job all wired, like ready to go, and then still make it to class after.\n\nIt was weird because normally if I sleep like that, I’m useless. But then I was just… up. And now it’s different, like I either can’t fall asleep or I wake up before my alarm and feel like I didn’t rest at all. My alarm is one of those annoying ones too, the kind that makes you want to throw your phone, but lately I’m already awake before it even starts.\n\nDoctor: During those weeks where you felt way better, did you ever feel like you were engaging in risky behaviors or activities that you might not normally do?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say “risky,” exactly, but I guess I’m not totally sure what counts.\n\nLike, I wasn’t out doing anything wild on purpose or anything. Mostly I was just busy and feeling like I could handle everything. Sometimes people act like just being out late is risky, but with work and school, late is kind of normal for me. My uncle used to say tradespeople keep vampire hours sometimes, which isn’t really true, but—sorry.\n\nI guess I don’t have a clear example of something I did that I’d call risky.\n\nDoctor: When you felt way better during those weeks, was it like a switch had been flipped or did it come on more gradually?\n\nPatient: It felt kind of like a switch, yeah. Not like one exact second, maybe, but I’d realize I was in it and suddenly everything felt bigger and better, like Miami was mine or something.\n\nAnd then at work, if somebody slowed me down or made some little comment, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. Which is funny because some of those guys talk all day anyway—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yeah, it didn’t feel like my normal mood gradually improving. It felt more like I’d flipped into this other mode for a few weeks.\n\nDoctor: After those weeks where you felt way better, did you always end up feeling really down or back to your normal depressive symptoms afterwards?\n\nPatient: Yeah… not always like right away, like I crashed the next morning or something. But I’ve been in this down stretch for, I don’t know, about nine months now.\n\nAt first I could kind of cover it, I guess. Like you learn how to look busy at work, and with school you can sort of tell yourself you’ll catch up later—though “later” turns into this whole pile of stuff. Sorry, I’m getting off track. It just got harder to hide once work and school started slipping.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed for most of every day over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, for the last two weeks, yes, pretty much most of the day. But it’s not just two weeks, it’s been this whole stretch for about nine months now.\n\nI was kind of able to keep it looking normal for a while, like just push through and act like I was handling things, but once school and work started slipping, it got harder to hide.\n\nDoctor: Did your periods of feeling way better and having a lot of energy always come after a period of feeling really down?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it always came after being really down. It’s hard for me to line it up perfectly. I just know there were these weeks where I felt almost too good, like Miami was mine, and I had all this energy.\n\nAnd then if someone at work slowed me down or made some little comment, I’d snap way harder than I normally would. Which, I mean, some coworkers do make it impossible to get anything done—but yeah, I’m not sure it was always right after a low period.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
